The paragraph in Part III (Scope, and a question that can be wrong):

"A contract is a declared subset of the physically admitted changes, and a stated scope is what makes it one. What makes a restriction appropriate to the question asked is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim; the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it."

So f11 L161 s1 = "A contract is a declared subset of the physically admitted changes, and a stated scope is what makes it one." s2 = "An account at a stated scope answers the question asked at that scope; it does not answer a broader question that failed, and a narrowing adopted after a failure is a new claim at a new index (Part VIII)." s3 = "What makes a restriction appropriate to the question asked is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim; the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it."

**O5 deep dive.**

Fixed verdict points:
(1) "This is a legitimate narrowing." — finding: the narrowing (to rooms warmer than twenty degrees) is legitimate.
(2) "The limit follows from a part of her account." — finding: the limit follows from a part of her account.

File 11 relevant text:
- L161 s1–s3 (Part III, Scope paragraph).
- L514 (Declared inputs).
- Possibly L259 (non-vacuity: stated scope) and the account machinery (Part II signatures, Part VI support).

Question: Does file 11's verdict reach "legitimate"?

Reading the theory as it stands: The theory has no positive criterion of legitimate restriction. It says: (i) a contract is a declared subset; a stated scope makes it one; (ii) an account at a stated scope answers the question at that scope, and a narrowing after a failure is a new claim at a new index; (iii) what makes a restriction appropriate is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim; the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it.

And L514: "the scope of a contract and what makes a restriction appropriate" are declared inputs the semantics records and does not supply; "A verdict that depends on one of these is a verdict given the input; where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled and the semantics says so rather than choosing the input from the verdict wanted."

Now: for O5, is "what makes a restriction appropriate" supplied by the case? The case supplies Greta's ground: the yeast works slowly in the cold. And the account exhibits it. And the counterfactual contrast (Tuesday limit has no ground). So the case supplies the appropriateness-ground.

Given the ground, what verdict does the theory reach? The theory says: a verdict that depends on this input is "a verdict given the input". So the theory issues a verdict given the ground. What verdict? Presumably the verdict on the point in question — the legitimacy of the narrowing. And the verdict, given the ground, is... hmm.

I think there's a real gap here: L514 tells us the verdict's status ("given the input") and its absence-condition ("missing → unsettled"), but no sentence in the theory says what the verdict IS when the input is present. The determination fills the gap: "The verdict given it is that the restriction is appropriate, because the limit follows from a part of her account." But where does "because the limit follows from a part of her account" come from as a criterion of appropriateness? From the fixed verdict's own reasoning, not from the theory. Hmm! Actually wait — the case says: "her account says why: the yeast that makes the gas works slowly in the cold" and "Replace her limit with 'on days other than Tuesdays' and her account has nothing to say about why Tuesdays would matter." So the case itself presents the account-following as what distinguishes the legitimate limit. Setup: The theory of explanation (file 11) is a formal semantics. We ask: does it, read as it stand, reach the verdict "Greta's narrowing to >20°C is legitimate" on a case where she justifies it by her account (yeast in the cold)?

The theory's relevant content:
1. Contracts are declared subsets of physically admitted edits; a stated scope makes a contract one. (Scope-honesty: state your exclusions.)
2. An account at a stated scope answers the question at that scope; a narrowing after a failure is a new claim at a new index.
3. What makes a restriction appropriate is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim; the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule certifying it.
4. Appropriateness (with scope) is a declared input; verdicts depending on it are given the input; where the input is missing, unsettled.

The SPLIT definition: "the theory's sentences support two readings that reach different findings, and the text leaves the choice between them open. Give both readings, each with its sentence." So if one reading says "legitimate" (L514's verdict-given-input) and the other says "the semantics certifies nothing, so the legitimacy is not established" (L161 s3), and the text leaves the choice open, then SPLIT.

But wait — is the choice really open? A careful reader must decide. L514 says verdicts are given the input; L161 s3 says no certifying rule. Hmm. OK, let me try to settle this by looking at the *function* of L514's sentence in the document. Part XIV, "Declared inputs": "Besides the two primitives, some claims take stated inputs that the semantics records and does not supply: [three families]. A verdict that depends on one of these is a verdict given the input; where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled and the semantics says so rather than choosing the input from the verdict wanted."

Function: to police verdicts. The semantics' verdicts that depend on declared inputs are indexed "given the input"; and where the input is missing, the semantics reports "unsettled" rather than picking an input to get the verdict wanted. So this is a *discipline* on verdicts: don't issue input-dependent verdicts without the input; when you do issue them, mark them as input-relative.

So the sentence is about *when the semantics' verdicts hold*: they hold given the input; they're unsettled without it. It's not a verdict-generator.

Hmm!!! So for O5: the semantics' verdict on the narrowing's legitimacy depends on the appropriateness-input. The input is present (the claim's ground). So the verdict is "a verdict given the input". Hmm — so the verdict holds given the input. The verdict being: "the restriction is legitimate"? Hmm — what generates that verdict? The claim's substantive part (L161 s3's identity: what makes a restriction appropriate is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim). Hmm.

Hmm. OK so the question remains: does L161 s3 generate the verdict?

E.g., "a restriction is certified when it follows from the account's components" — that would be a certifying rule. The semantics supplies no such rule. Hmm!!! So the semantics does NOT have the rule "a restriction grounded in the account is legitimate". So the theory cannot reach "legitimate" from "the limit follows from a part of her account"!!! Hmm!!!

Hmm — hmm! But the counter: the disclaimer means the theory does not *find* the action right; it finds that rightness is standard-grounded and that the standard is present. Hmm. Hmm — "What makes a restriction appropriate... is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim" — hmm, "what makes X is Y" typically means "Y is what makes X" = "Y makes X". Hmm — but "what makes X is Y" can also be read as "the nature of X-making is Y" — e.g., "what makes a joke funny is its incongruity" = incongruity makes jokes funny. Hmm — so it's a general claim: X-making consists in Y. So: restrictions are made appropriate by substantive parts of claims. Greta's is. So appropriate. ✓✓.

Hmm — OK. And "the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it" — hmm. OK. Hmm. Let me also steelman the SILENT case one final time, focusing on the word "certifies":

The fixed verdict's point 1 is a verdict of legitimacy. "Legitimate" = sanctioned, proper, valid. For the theory to reach "legitimate", it must have a rule under which the restriction counts as proper. The theory says it "supplies no rule that certifies it" — i.e., it has no rule under which a restriction is established as proper. It records the restriction and leaves it to criticism. **O30:**
Point 1 (most serious): Auditor 1's SPLIT — f11 L419 s2's first clause ("Work supplied from outside that boundary... remains an outside contribution however it is executed inside") against its second ("a process that runs inside the boundary is the system's own today whoever wrote it"), for a routine uploaded that morning. Full strength: the routine is work supplied from outside, executed inside; the first clause seems to reach "outside contribution" for the comparison that executes it. Why it fails: the clauses take different subjects — the supplied work (the routine's writing/product, which "remains an outside contribution", matching "the origin of the routine is the person's") and the process that runs inside (matching "Ownership of today's history is the robot's" and "the robot's work today"). The fixed verdict is built on the same split ("Ownership of today's history is the robot's; the origin of the routine is the person's"), so the two clauses reach different points rather than different findings on one point. Independently, the determination's reductio holds: if external authorship made a run "work supplied from outside", the second clause's "whoever wrote it" would have no application, and O17's maker-written routine would fall with it.

Full strength. SILENT's first ground requires "an input the case leaves unstated"; it is stated here.

## O5 — Greta's dough

**1. Tried: f11 L161 s3 withholds the finding.** Both 1C readings rest here.1C A: "The quoted sentences leave the legitimacy of a restriction to a criticizable input with no certifying rule, so the theory stops short of the fixed verdict's finding." Put at full strength: the fixed verdict's first point is a verdict of legitimacy — "This is a legitimate narrowing" — and f11 L161 s3 ends "the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it". Read "certifies" as "decides", and the theory's sentences stop short on point 1: SILENT's second ground. That reading is why four of five readings go the other way.

Why it fails. Three things, in order of weight. (a) f11 L514 is the rule that says when a verdict on this point is unsettled: "A verdict that depends on one of these is a verdict given the input; where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled …" The declared input it names is "the scope of a contract and what makes a restriction appropriate (Part III)". The case states it: "her account says why: the yeast that makes the gas works slowly in the cold." So the verdict is "given the input", not unsettled. For "unsettled" to be the right mark on O5, the present/missing contrast in L514 would have to make no difference to the mark on any appropriateness point, and "a verdict that depends on one of these" would have no case on Part III. SILENT's first ground — "an input the case leaves unstated" — is unavailable for the same reason. (b) The first clause of f11 L161 s3 is positive: "What makes a restriction appropriate to the question asked is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim." Greta's appropriateness-maker is exactly that — the account's yeast component, the part her limit follows from. So the theory's verdict on the point is that the narrowing is appropriate in the way the theory describes, with "uncertified and open to criticism" as the limit. "Same finding" admits "a qualification, a limit or a more exact wording". (c) The file-10 parallel is decisive against reading "certifies" as "decides": f10 L41 polices restrictions by "the requirement that the exclusion be stated, and then by any criticism that supplies the excluded change" — no certifying rule in file 10 either — and the file-10 mark is AGREE (baseline; both 1K AGREE). If the absence of a certifying rule withheld the finding, O5 would turn on material the two files share, not on the new sentences; the ruled passage (M17, M48) would be wrong in the other direction. Also compare Part XI: "Their declaration makes no claim that the aims are worth pursuing" accompanies a (P) that still returns repair verdicts given the declared obligations. The document's pattern is condition-plus-disclaimer, and the disclaimer limits force, not content.

**2. Tried: L514 gives a verdict's status, not its content.** 1C B: "The theory lists what makes a restriction appropriate among its declared inputs and certifies none, so the legitimacy of this narrowing is left open." The strongest form: even if a verdict issues, it must be produced by the theory's definitions and conditions, and none of them says "a limit that follows from a part of the account is legitimate". Why it fails: f11 L161 s3's first clause is the condition — appropriateness consists in a substantive, criticizable part of the claim — and the case supplies one. The condition does the same work for the Tuesday counterfactual: "her account has nothing to say about why Tuesdays would matter" removes the appropriateness-maker. The theory therefore draws the very contrast the case is built on.

**3. Tried: the stated-scope sentence cannot separate the two limits.** 1C B rejects the AGREE reading through f11 L259 (= f10 L272) because "Tuesdays" would satisfy the stated-scope requirement too. The observation is correct and the determination agrees with it in substance — but that sentence is not the route. The route is f11 L161 s3 with the account's component (the signature that changes under temperature, in the words of f10 L140). Note also that f11 L259 is shared word for word with f10 L272, so it cannot make the passage CHANGED; the new work is done by M17 and M48.

**4. Checked and upheld: the non-circular-dependence route.** Auditor 1 on B reached AGREE through f11 L257. The determination rejects it: "A Tuesday limit would leave the yeast block removable too, so that condition does not tell the two limits apart." I re-read the condition — an edit that removes a block of Γ and changes the answer profile — and the rejection holds: it is a property of the account's internals and is indifferent to which limit is stated.

**5. The rest of the row.** File 10: AGREE is fixed — S75's baseline and both 1K returns agree, so the correction rule has nothing to correct (and f10 L41, L140, L272 support the mark as the determination records). Verdict SAME, passage CHANGED (additive) at f11 L161 s3 and L514, no direction and no kind to rule: correct as tabled. I needed no file-10 text beyond section 4. The residual hazard is real and the determination records it: if "certifies" were read as "decides", f11 L161 s3 and f11 L514 would pull in opposite directions and the row would fall to SILENT or SPLIT. The texts, read together, do not support that.

O5: RULING STANDS

## O30 — The routine uploaded that morning

**1. Tried: f11 L419 s2 supports two readings (SPLIT).** Auditor 1's argument at full strength: the first clause — "Work supplied from outside that boundary … remains an outside contribution however it is executed inside" — is aimed at exactly this configuration (an artifact supplied from outside and executed inside), while the second clause — "a process that runs inside the boundary is the system's own today whoever wrote it" — assigns today's run to the robot. For an uploaded routine the two pull apart, and, auditor 1 adds, "the case states no boundary to decide between them". If both readings are live, the mark is SPLIT and test (b) fails.

Why it fails. The clauses take different subjects and reach different points of the fixed verdict. The first clause's subject is the work supplied — the routine's writing and delivery — and what it preserves is the contribution's origin: "remains an outside contribution" is Part XI's credit vocabulary ("it credits each contribution the history establishes"), and it gives "the origin of the routine is the person's". The second clause's subject is "a process that runs inside the boundary", and it gives "The comparison … was the robot's work today" and "Ownership of today's history is the robot's". The fixed verdict is built on the same division — "Ownership of today's history is the robot's; the origin of the routine is the person's" — so there is one reading on which both clauses are true of different relata, and no choice left open. Independently, the determination's reductio survives scrutiny: if authorship outside the boundary made a run "work supplied from outside", the second clause's "whoever wrote it" would have no application to any externally written process, and O17's maker-written routine would fall with O30. The clause must cover processes written by others.

**2. Tried: the boundary input is missing (SILENT).** 1C A: "The theory's ownership verdict depends on a declared boundary that the case does not supply … 'itself' is the verdict's word, not a declaration." At full strength: f11 L465 — "a declared boundary (which processes and resources are the system's) … Both are declared before the attribution, not chosen after it" — plus f11 L514's "where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled", plus f11 L419 s2's warning that "where the boundary is drawn decides, not where the process sits in the casing". The case gives only "uploaded to it" and "inside" — location.

Why it fails. The deciding matter is stated in the situation, and SILENT's first ground requires an input "the case leaves unstated". The situation says "The robot compares the pressures itself" — "itself" is in the situation, and it places the comparison among the robot's own acts — and that the routine was "uploaded to it", i.e. into the robot. f11 L465's own gloss of the boundary is "which processes and resources are the system's", and the situation states that for the process at issue. The alternative the strict reading needs — a boundary consistent with the situation under which the comparison is not among the robot's processes — would contradict "compares the pressures itself". f11 L514's guard ("rather than choosing the input from the verdict wanted") is satisfied by taking the case's attribution rather than a drawn one. The casing clause of f11 L419 s2 does real work where a boundary is stated against the casing (the determination's O41 and O51); nothing in O30 does that.

**3. The positive match, which the same sentences give.** f11 L419 s2's "is the system's own today" is the verdict's "was the robot's work today" and "Ownership of today's history is the robot's"; "whover wrote it" is "Who wrote the routine is a separate fact, and a true one"; and f11 L401 s3 — "Reconstruction by a learner is construction; relay is not." — covers the routine's arrival by upload. The determination's reading of the first clause's list ("a diagnosis, a decisive question, an instruction about what to read") as handed-in results is one harmonisation; the relata reading in point 1 is another; either removes the SPLIT, and point 1 does not depend on which is adopted.

**4. The rest of the row.** File 10 AGREE (baseline; both 1K), verdict SAME, passage CHANGED (additive) at f11 L419 s2 and L465 (M37, M44), direction and kind not engaged: correct as tabled. I needed no file-10 text beyond section 4. The boundary hazard the determination records is the same as at O17 and is answered the same way.

O30: RULING STANDS

Now O17:

## O17 — The robot's log and the maker's manual

**1. Tried: the log shows only the casing, and the boundary is undeclared (SILENT).** This is the strongest challenge of the three rows, and1C A and auditor 1's blind reading both rest on it. At full strength: the fixed verdict's first sentence grounds ownership in "the log, which shows the routine running inside the robot" — a location fact. f11 L419 s2 says the opposite kind of fact decides: "where the boundary is drawn decides, not where the process sits in the casing." f11 L467 s3 grounds ownership in "the processes and resources the boundary includes", and f11 L465 says "Both are declared before the attribution, not chosen after it". The case names no boundary; what it gives is the routine's physical placement. With f11 L514 ("where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled"), the verdict on point1 is unsettled — and the determination's "given that the robot's boundary includes the routine" is not a qualification under "Same finding" but the missing input itself, which L514 makes the verdict unsettled rather than qualified. That is SILENT's first ground exactly.

Why it fails. (a) The finding on all three points of the fixed verdict is a contrast of grounds, and f11 L467 s3 gives the contrast almost word for word: "Ownership is grounded in the processes and resources the boundary includes, never in the capability being attributed: 'owned because it can, and can because owned' grounds neither." Points 2 and 3 — "Strike the manual's sentence and nothing is lost" and "keep only the manual's sentence and there is nothing" — are reached outright ("never in the capability being attributed"; "grounds neither"), and the case's manual is the theory's example in its own words ("because the robot can diagnose" is "owned because it can"). Point 1 is reached as the same finding in more exact wording: ownership rests on the process the log evidences, with "the boundary includes" as the exact ground instead of "running inside the robot". "Same finding" sends that difference to NOTE. (b) The deciding matter is stated: "A repair robot keeps a log of every comparison it runs" attributes the comparisons to the robot, and the routine is shown producing today's diagnosis inside it. The matter the case leaves open is not the boundary but the ground of ownership — the manual does not dispute that the routine is the robot's own, only why. SILENT's first ground again requires the case to leave the input unstated. (c) The casing clause bites where a declared boundary diverges from the case's description (O41, O51); here nothing sets a boundary apart from the robot, so reading "the robot" as the robot is the case's own statement.

**2. Tried: the qualification is load-bearing, so the row is really SILENT.** If one insists that f11 L465's "declared" means stated in the case in so many words, then the boundary is missing and f11 L514 unsettles the verdict on every ownership row without a stated boundary — O30 and O17 both. That reading cannot be dismissed: it is what "the row's substance is exposed" in 04d records. It fails because it makes the theory's ownership conditions dead letters exactly where the theory's own contrast examples (the remote author of O30, the maker of O17) are meant to apply, and because the cases' attributions state the boundary in the only sense the definition requires ("which processes and resources are the system's"). Had this held, the mark would be file 11 SILENT and the row a theory change away through f11 L419, L465, L467 s3.

**3. The rest of the row.** File 10 AGREE (baseline; both 1K AGREE; f10 L476 leaves "owned" undefined and the ordinary sense gives the same contrast). Verdict SAME, passage CHANGED (additive) at f11 L467 s3 and L419 s1 (M45, M37) — correct, since f10 L476 is f11 L467 s1–s2 and the ground-contrast sentence is new. Direction and kind are not engaged. No file-10 text beyond section 4 was needed.
